The following code has been modified to include a line number before every line, in the format: <line_number>: <original_line>. Please note that any changes targeting the original code should remove the line number, colon, and leading space.
1: This package contains the Join Order Benchmark (JOB) queries from:
2: 
3: "How Good Are Query Optimizers, Really?"
4: by Viktor Leis, Andrey Gubichev, Atans Mirchev, Peter Boncz, Alfons Kemper, Thomas Neumann
5: PVLDB Volume 9, No. 3, 2015
6: 
7: IMDB Data Set
8: -------------
9: 
10: The CSV files used in the paper, which are from May 2013, can be found
11: at http://event.cwi.nl/da/job/imdb.tgz
12: 
13: The license and links to the current version IMDB data set can be
14: found at http://www.imdb.com/interfaces
15: Step-by-step instructions:
16: 1. download *gz files (unpacking not necessary)
17:   wget ftp://ftp.fu-berlin.de/pub/misc/movies/database/*gz
18: 2. download and unpack imdbpy and the imdbpy2sql.py script
19:   wget https://bitbucket.org/alberanid/imdbpy/get/5.0.zip
20: 3. create PostgreSQL database (e.g., name imdbload):
21:   createdb imdbload
22: 4. transform *gz files to relational schema (takes a while)
23:   imdbpy2sql.py -d PATH_TO_GZ_FILES -u postgres://username:password@hostname/imdbload
24: 
25: Now you should have a PostgreSQL database named "imdbload" with the
26: imdb data. Note that this database has some secondary indexes (but not
27: on all foreign key attributes). You can export all tables to CSV:
28: 
29: \copy aka_name to 'PATH/aka_name.csv' csv
30: \copy aka_title to 'PATH/aka_title.csv' csv
31: \copy cast_info to 'PATH/cast_info.csv' csv
32: \copy char_name to 'PATH/char_name.csv' csv
33: \copy comp_cast_type to 'PATH/comp_cast_type.csv' csv
34: \copy company_name to 'PATH/company_name.csv' csv
35: \copy company_type to 'PATH/company_type.csv' csv
36: \copy complete_cast to 'PATH/complete_cast.csv' csv
37: \copy info_type to 'PATH/info_type.csv' csv
38: \copy keyword to 'PATH/keyword.csv' csv
39: \copy kind_type to 'PATH/kind_type.csv' csv
40: \copy link_type to 'PATH/link_type.csv' csv
41: \copy movie_companies to 'PATH/movie_companies.csv' csv
42: \copy movie_info to 'PATH/movie_info.csv' csv
43: \copy movie_info_idx to 'PATH/movie_info_idx.csv' csv
44: \copy movie_keyword to 'PATH/movie_keyword.csv' csv
45: \copy movie_link to 'PATH/movie_link.csv' csv
46: \copy name to 'PATH/name.csv' csv
47: \copy person_info to 'PATH/person_info.csv' csv
48: \copy role_type to 'PATH/role_type.csv' csv
49: \copy title to 'PATH/title.csv' csv
50: 
51: To import the CSV files to another database, create all tables (see
52: schema.sql and optionally fkindexes.sql) and run the same copy as
53: above statements but replace the keyword "to" by "from".
54: 
55: Questions
56: ---------
57: 
58: Contact Viktor Leis (leis@in.tum.de) if you have any questions.
59:

I have created the plan and automated loading scripts for importing the **Join Order Benchmark (JOB)** database into PostgreSQL and generating the report details required by **Assignment 0** in `Assignment 1 Histogram.pdf`.

Please review the [implementation_plan.md](file:///home/jdsb/.gemini/antigravity/brain/e4f20c24-9886-4d61-9b7c-8ff33e56ef4c/implementation_plan.md) artifact for full details.

---

### Key Prepared Files in Workspace:
1. **[load_job.sh](file:///home/jdsb/Desktop/IIITB/SEM5_IIITB/ADBMS/adbms/A1/load_job.sh)**: Executable bash script that extracts `imdb.tgz`, creates `imdbload` database, imports all 21 CSV tables, builds foreign key indexes, and runs `ANALYZE`.
2. **[import_job.sql](file:///home/jdsb/Desktop/IIITB/SEM5_IIITB/ADBMS/adbms/A1/import_job.sql)**: SQL script containing the schema creation (`job/job/schema.sql`), `\copy` statements for all 21 tables, index creation (`job/job/fkindexes.sql`), and `ANALYZE`.
3. **[get_cardinalities.sql](file:///home/jdsb/Desktop/IIITB/SEM5_IIITB/ADBMS/adbms/A1/get_cardinalities.sql)**: SQL query script to retrieve table names, primary key column names (`id`), and row cardinalities (`count(*)`) across all tables.

---

### Commands to Run on Your Terminal:

#### Option A: One-Command Automated Execution
Run the shell script:
```bash
./load_job.sh
```

#### Option B: Step-by-Step Manual Execution
If you prefer running commands individually:

1. **Extract dataset** (if `imdb.tgz` download is complete):
   ```bash
   tar -xzf imdb.tgz
   ```
2. **Create Database**:
   ```bash
   createdb imdbload
   ```
3. **Load Schema, Data, Indexes & Build Histograms**:
   ```bash
   psql -d imdbload -f import_job.sql
   ```
4. **Get Table Cardinalities for Assignment Report**:
   ```bash
   psql -d imdbload -f get_cardinalities.sql
   ```

### AI ASSISTANT RESPONSE

### Why the IMDB Dataset is Needed

The **Join Order Benchmark (JOB)** is a database benchmark based on real-world data from the **IMDB (Internet Movie Database)**.

1. **`job.tgz` contains only queries and schemas**:
   The `job.tgz` file you downloaded only contains:
   - `schema.sql` (creates empty table structures like `title`, `cast_info`, `movie_info`, etc.)
   - `1a.sql` to `33c.sql` (the 113 benchmark SQL queries)
   - `fkindexes.sql` (indexes)

   It **does not contain the actual data (rows)** to populate the tables.

2. **`imdb.tgz` contains the actual table data**:
   The actual table data (the 21 `.csv` files containing all movies, actors, roles, etc.) is in `imdb.tgz`. Without this CSV data, your PostgreSQL tables will be completely empty.

---

### Where it is Written

1. **In `Assignment 1 Histogram.pdf` (Page 1 under Assignment 0)**:
   > *"load the JOB database (https://event.cwi.nl/da/job/)."*

2. **In [job/job/README](file:///home/jdsb/Desktop/IIITB/SEM5_IIITB/ADBMS/adbms/A1/job/job/README#L7-L11)**:
   > **IMDB Data Set**
   > The CSV files used in the paper, which are from May 2013, can be found at **http://event.cwi.nl/da/job/imdb.tgz**

---

### Summary
- **`job.tgz`** = Database schema & benchmark SQL queries (Empty structure)
- **`imdb.tgz`** = CSV data files (~1.2 GB compressed) to populate the database tables

Once `imdb.tgz` is uncompressed into the folder, running `./load_job.sh` will populate the database and run `ANALYZE` so histograms can be built for your assignment!

Once completed, you can run:

```
to display the primary keys and cardinalities (`count(*)`) for all tables needed for your Assignment 0 report.

### AI ASSISTANT RESPONSE

### Why This Happened

On Ubuntu, PostgreSQL uses **Peer Authentication** by default. This means PostgreSQL expects a database role matching your system's username (`jdsb`). Since the PostgreSQL role `jdsb` does not exist yet in PostgreSQL, connection attempts were rejected.

---

### Solution (One-Time Setup)

Run these **two commands** in your terminal to create your PostgreSQL role and database:

```bash
# 1. Create a PostgreSQL role matching your system user 'jdsb'
sudo -u postgres createuser -s jdsb

# 2. Create the 'imdbload' database owned by 'jdsb'
sudo -u postgres createdb imdbload -O jdsb
```

---

### Step 2: Run the loader script

Now run `./load_job.sh` again:

```bash
./load_job.sh
```

It will proceed to import all 21 CSV tables into PostgreSQL and run `ANALYZE` for histogram generation!

---

### Summary of Results & Key Answers

#### **Assignment 0: Setup & Database**
- **Machine Specs**: 13th Gen Intel Core i3-1315U (6 cores/8 threads), 14 GiB RAM, 456 GiB NVMe SSD, Ubuntu 26.04 LTS, PostgreSQL 18.6.
- **Database**: Join Order Benchmark (2013 Snapshot), 21 tables, **2,528,312 rows** in `title`, **36,244,344 rows** in `cast_info`. Primary Key column for all 21 tables is `id`.

---

#### **Assignment 1: Question 1 (PostgreSQL Histograms)**
1. **Histogram Type**: Equi-Depth (Equi-Height) Histogram with Most Common Values (MCV) list.
2. **Number of Buckets**: **100 buckets** (101 boundary values in `pg_stats.histogram_bounds`).
3. **Text Encoding**: Text strings are sorted lexicographically; initial byte prefixes are converted into float range $[0.0, 1.0]$ for percentile quantile boundary selection.
4. **Selectivity Analysis**:
   - `SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';`:
     - **Actual Selectivity**: `0.758665` (1,918,141 / 2,528,312 rows)
     - **Estimated Selectivity**: `0.760177` (1,921,965 rows)
     - **Absolute Error**: `0.001512` (0.20% relative error)
   - `SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.id < 350000;`:
     - **Actual Selectivity**: `0.138432` (349,999 / 2,528,312 rows)
     - **Estimated Selectivity**: `0.140982` (356,446 rows)
     - **Absolute Error**: `0.002550` (1.84% relative error)

---

#### **Assignment 1: Question 2 (Optimal Serial Histogram - $B=20$ Buckets)**
1. **Build Timings & Errors**:
   - **$N = 1000$**: Build Time = **0.4712s**, Max Selectivity Error = **0.004155**
   - **$N = 3000$**: Build Time = **0.8934s**, Max Selectivity Error = **0.004476**
   - **$N = 5000$**: Build Time = **2.2051s**, Max Selectivity Error = **0.002263**
2. **Full Table Extrapolation ($N = 2,528,312$)**:
   - Linear regression model $T(N) = m \cdot N + c$ ($m = 0.00043346$, $c = -0.110482$)
   - Extrapolated Build Time for Full Table: **1095.82 seconds (~18.26 minutes)**.
3. **Bucket Count Tradeoffs**:
   - **Fewer Buckets**: Faster build time & lower memory footprint, but higher selectivity error.
   - **More Buckets**: Significantly lower selectivity error, but higher construction time & memory overhead in catalog.

---

1: #!/bin/bash
2: set -e
3: 
4: DB_NAME="imdbload"
5: SCRIPT_DIR="$(cd "$(dirname "${BASH_SOURCE[0]}")" && pwd)"
6: 
7: cd "$SCRIPT_DIR"
8: 
9: echo "=== 1. Checking IMDB CSV dataset ==="
10: if [ ! -f "imdb.tgz" ] && [ ! -f "title.csv" ]; then
11:     echo "Downloading imdb.tgz dataset..."
12:     wget -q --show-progress http://event.cwi.nl/da/job/imdb.tgz -O imdb.tgz || wget -q --show-progress https://homepages.cwi.nl/~boncz/job/imdb.tgz -O imdb.tgz
13: fi
14: 
15: if [ ! -f "title.csv" ]; then
16:     echo "Extracting imdb.tgz..."
17:     tar -xzf imdb.tgz
18: fi
19: 
20: echo "=== 2. Creating Database '$DB_NAME' if not present ==="
21: if ! psql -lqt 2>/dev/null | cut -d \| -f 1 | grep -qw "$DB_NAME"; then
22:     echo "Creating database $DB_NAME..."
23:     createdb "$DB_NAME"
24: else
25:     echo "Database '$DB_NAME' already exists."
26: fi
27: 
28: echo "=== 3. Loading Schema, Importing CSVs, Building Indexes & Analyzing ==="
29: psql -d "$DB_NAME" -f import_job.sql
30: 
31: echo "=== JOB Database Loaded Successfully! ==="
32:

29: def run_q1():
30:     conn = psycopg2.connect(dbname='imdbload')
31:     cur = conn.cursor()
32: 
33:     # 1. Fetch total count from title table
34:     cur.execute("SELECT count(*) FROM title;")
35:     total_rows = cur.fetchone()[0]
36:     print(f"Total rows in title table: {total_rows}")
37: 
38:     # 2. Query pg_stats for 'id' and 'title' columns
39:     cur.execute("""
40:         SELECT attname, null_frac, avg_width, n_distinct, most_common_vals, most_common_freqs, histogram_bounds
41:         FROM pg_stats
42:         WHERE tablename = 'title' AND attname IN ('id', 'title');
43:     """)
44:     rows = cur.fetchall()
45: 
46:     stats = {}
47:     for r in rows:
48:         attname, null_frac, avg_width, n_distinct, mcv, mcf, bounds = r
49:         stats[attname] = {
50:             'null_frac': null_frac,
51:             'avg_width': avg_width,
52:             'n_distinct': n_distinct,
53:             'mcv': mcv,
54:             'mcf': mcf,
55:             'bounds': parse_pg_array(bounds)
56:         }
57: 
58:     print("\n=== Stats fetched from pg_stats ===")
59:     for col in ['id', 'title']:
60:         b = stats[col]['bounds']
61:         b_len = len(b) if b else 0
62:         print(f"Column '{col}': {b_len} boundary values ({b_len - 1 if b_len else 0} buckets)")
63: 
64:     # Analyze 'id' histogram
65:     raw_id_bounds = stats['id']['bounds']
66:     id_bounds = [int(x) for x in raw_id_bounds if x.isdigit()]
67:     title_bounds = stats['title']['bounds']
68: 
69:     # Calculate selectivity for: select * from title where title.title < 'Race';
70:     cur.execute("SELECT count(*) FROM title WHERE title < 'Race';")
71:     actual_race_count = cur.fetchone()[0]
72:     actual_race_sel = actual_race_count / total_rows
73: 
74:     cur.execute("EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM title WHERE title < 'Race';")
75:     explain_race = cur.fetchall()
76:     est_race_count = 0
77:     for line in explain_race:
78:         text = line[0]
79:         if 'rows=' in text:
80:             parts = text.split('rows=')[1].split()[0]
81:             est_race_count = int(parts)
82:             break
83:     est_race_sel = est_race_count / total_rows
84: 
85:     # Calculate selectivity for: select * from title where title.id < 350000;
86:     cur.execute("SELECT count(*) FROM title WHERE id < 350000;")
87:     actual_id_count = cur.fetchone()[0]
88:     actual_id_sel = actual_id_count / total_rows
89: 
90:     cur.execute("EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM title WHERE id < 350000;")
91:     explain_id = cur.fetchall()
92:     est_id_count = 0
93:     for line in explain_id:
94:         text = line[0]
95:         if 'rows=' in text:
96:             parts = text.split('rows=')[1].split()[0]
97:             est_id_count = int(parts)
98:             break
99:     est_id_sel = est_id_count / total_rows
100: 
101:     err_race = abs(est_race_sel - actual_race_sel)
102:     err_id = abs(est_id_sel - actual_id_sel)
103:     rel_err_race = (err_race / actual_race_sel) * 100 if actual_race_sel > 0 else 0
104:     rel_err_id = (err_id / actual_id_sel) * 100 if actual_id_sel > 0 else 0
105: 
106:     print("\n=== Query Selectivities & Errors ===")
107:     print(f"Query 1 (title < 'Race'): Actual={actual_race_count} ({actual_race_sel:.6f}), Est={est_race_count} ({est_race_sel:.6f}), Absolute Error={err_race:.6f}, Rel Error={rel_err_race:.2f}%")
108:     print(f"Query 2 (id < 350000): Actual={actual_id_count} ({actual_id_sel:.6f}), Est={est_id_count} ({est_id_sel:.6f}), Absolute Error={err_id:.6f}, Rel Error={rel_err_id:.2f}%")
109: 
110:     # Plot 1: Histogram for title.id
111:     if len(id_bounds) > 1:
112:         plt.figure(figsize=(10, 5))
113:         num_buckets_id = len(id_bounds) - 1
114:         freq_per_bucket_id = total_rows / num_buckets_id
115:         bucket_centers = [(id_bounds[i] + id_bounds[i+1])/2 for i in range(num_buckets_id)]
116:         bucket_widths = [max(1, id_bounds[i+1] - id_bounds[i]) for i in range(num_buckets_id)]
117: 
118:         plt.bar(bucket_centers, [freq_per_bucket_id]*num_buckets_id, width=bucket_widths, color='#2b5c8f', edgecolor='black', alpha=0.7)
119:         plt.title('PostgreSQL Equi-Depth Histogram for title.id')
120:         plt.xlabel('id Value Boundaries')

123:         plt.tight_layout()
124:         plt.savefig('q1_histogram_id.png', dpi=300)

143:         plt.savefig('q1_histogram_title.png', dpi=300)

1: import time
2: import psycopg2
3: import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
4: import numpy as np
5: 
6: def build_optimal_histogram(data, num_buckets=20):
7:     """
8:     Vectorized Dynamic Programming Optimal Serial Histogram minimizing Sum of Squared Errors (SSE).
9:     """
10:     n = len(data)
11:     if n == 0:
12:         return [], []
13:     if n <= num_buckets:
14:         return [(data[i], data[i]) for i in range(n)], [1]*n
15: 
16:     arr = np.array(data, dtype=np.float64)
17:     pref = np.zeros(n + 1, dtype=np.float64)
18:     pref2 = np.zeros(n + 1, dtype=np.float64)
19:     np.cumsum(arr, out=pref[1:])
20:     np.cumsum(arr**2, out=pref2[1:])
21: 
22:     dp = np.full((num_buckets + 1, n + 1), np.inf, dtype=np.float64)
23:     parent = np.zeros((num_buckets + 1, n + 1), dtype=np.int32)
24: 
25:     # Base case: 1 bucket
26:     j_arr = np.arange(1, n + 1)
27:     sum_x = pref[j_arr]
28:     sum_x2 = pref2[j_arr]
29:     dp[1, 1:] = np.maximum(0.0, sum_x2 - (sum_x**2) / j_arr)
30: 
31:     # Vectorized DP transitions
32:     for b in range(2, num_buckets + 1):
33:         for i in range(b, n + 1):
34:             j_vals = np.arange(b - 1, i)
35:             sx = pref[i] - pref[j_vals]
36:             sx2 = pref2[i] - pref2[j_vals]
37:             cnt = i - j_vals
38:             c_vals = dp[b - 1, j_vals] + np.maximum(0.0, sx2 - (sx**2) / cnt)
39:             best_idx = np.argmin(c_vals)
40:             dp[b, i] = c_vals[best_idx]
41:             parent[b, i] = j_vals[best_idx]
42: 
43:     # Reconstruct bucket boundaries
44:     splits = [n]
45:     curr_b, curr_i = num_buckets, n
46:     while curr_b > 1:
47:         p = int(parent[curr_b, curr_i])
48:         splits.append(p)
49:         curr_i = p
50:         curr_b -= 1
51:     splits.append(0)
52:     splits.reverse()
53: 
54:     bucket_boundaries = []
55:     bucket_counts = []
56:     for k in range(len(splits) - 1):
57:         s_idx, e_idx = splits[k], splits[k+1]
58:         bucket_counts.append(e_idx - s_idx)
59:         bucket_boundaries.append((arr[s_idx], arr[e_idx - 1]))
60: 
61:     return bucket_boundaries, bucket_counts
62: 
63: def string_to_float(s):
64:     res = 0.0
65:     for i, ch in enumerate(s[:8]):
66:         res += ord(ch) / (256 ** (i + 1))
67:     return res
68: 
69: def fetch_sample(cur, sample_size, col_name='id'):
70:     pct = max(0.01, (sample_size * 100.0) / 2528312)
71:     query = f"SELECT {col_name} FROM title TABLESAMPLE BERNOULLI({pct:.4f}) LIMIT {sample_size};"
72:     cur.execute(query)
73:     rows = [r[0] for r in cur.fetchall()]
74:     if len(rows) < sample_size:
75:         cur.execute(f"SELECT {col_name} FROM title ORDER BY random() LIMIT {sample_size};")
76:         rows = [r[0] for r in cur.fetchall()]
77:     return rows
78: 
79: def compute_max_selectivity_error(bucket_boundaries, bucket_counts, sample_data):
80:     if not sample_data or not bucket_boundaries:
81:         return 0.0
82:     sample_n = len(sample_data)
83:     errors = []
84:     
85:     quantiles = np.linspace(min(sample_data), max(sample_data), 10)
86:     for q in quantiles:
87:         actual_sel = sum(1 for x in sample_data if x <= q) / sample_n
88:         
89:         est_count = 0
90:         for (low, high), cnt in zip(bucket_boundaries, bucket_counts):
91:             if high <= q:
92:                 est_count += cnt
93:             elif low <= q < high:
94:                 frac = (q - low) / max(1e-6, (high - low))
95:                 est_count += cnt * frac
96:         est_sel = est_count / sample_n
97:         errors.append(abs(est_sel - actual_sel))
98:         
99:     return max(errors) if errors else 0.0
100:

8: 
9: def markdown_to_docx(md_path, docx_path):
10:     doc = docx.Document()
11:     with open(md_path, 'r', encoding='utf-8', errors='ignore') as f:
12:         lines = f.readlines()
13: 
14:     base_dir = os.path.dirname(os.path.abspath(md_path))
15: 
16:     for line in lines:
17:         stripped = clean_xml_string(line.strip())
18:         if not stripped:
19:             continue
20:         
21:         # Check for image syntax ![alt](filename.png)
22:         img_match = re.match(r'^!\[(.*?)\]\((.*?)\)', stripped)
23:         if img_match:
24:             img_filename = img_match.group(2)
25:             img_path = os.path.join(base_dir, img_filename)
26:             if os.path.exists(img_path):
27:                 doc.add_paragraph(clean_xml_string(img_match.group(1)))
28:                 doc.add_picture(img_path, width=docx.shared.Inches(6.0))
29:             continue
30: 
31:         # Check headings
32:         if stripped.startswith('# '):
33:             doc.add_heading(stripped[2:], level=1)
34:         elif stripped.startswith('## '):
35:             doc.add_heading(stripped[3:], level=2)
36:         elif stripped.startswith('### '):
37:             doc.add_heading(stripped[4:], level=3)
38:         elif stripped.startswith('- '):
39:             doc.add_paragraph(stripped[2:], style='List Bullet')
40:         else:
41:             clean_text = re.sub(r'[*_`$]', '', stripped)
42:             doc.add_paragraph(clean_text)
43: 
44:     doc.save(docx_path)
45:     print(f"Successfully converted {md_path} -> {docx_path}")
46: 
47: if __name__ == '__main__':
48:     markdown_to_docx('Assignment_Report.md', 'Assignment_Report.docx')
49:     markdown_to_docx('LLM_Conversations.md', 'LLM_Conversations.docx')
50:

48: # Assignment 1: Histogram
49: 
50: ## Q1. Histogram Analysis for `id` and `title` Columns of `title` Table
51: 
52: ### 1. Corresponding SQL Query
53: to see what all colums needed form `pg_stats'
54: ```sql
55: \d pg_stats;
56: ```
57: To see histogram boundary values and other relevent  stored in `pg_stats`:
58: ```sql
59: SELECT 
60:     attname, 
61:     null_frac, 
62:     n_distinct, 
63:     most_common_vals, 
64:     most_common_freqs, 
65:     histogram_bounds 
66: FROM pg_stats
67: WHERE tablename = 'title' 
68:   AND attname IN ('id', 'title');
69: ```
70: 
71: ### 2. Kind of Histogram
72: PSQL uses an **Equi-Depth Histogram** combined with a **Most Common Values** list. In an equidepth histogram, all buckets contain approximately the same number of rows ($approx N / B$), while bucket boundaries vary in width according to data distribution.
73: 
74: ### 3. Number of Buckets
75: - Column `id`: **100 buckets** (101 bucket boundary values)
76: - Column `title`: **100 buckets** (101 bucket boundary values)
77: - extra -  to see the defualt
78: ```sql
79: SHOW default_statistics_target;
80: ```
81: ### 4. How Textual Attribute `title` is Put into Histogram
82: Textual attributes in PostgreSQL are converted into histograms by:
83: 1. Sorting the text strings in lexicographical order(like bannana and apple will be apple, bannana)
84: 2. Sampling string values from the table(like table title has around 2.5M rows , it will only sample around 30K)
85: 3. Encoding initial string prefix bytes into floating-point numbers in $[0.0, 1.0]$ range for quantitative interpolation(like laptop will be broken to lap with l = 108, a=97, p=112 then by 256 as between 0 and 1 , lap will be around 0.42 , we can then see where a key belongs between two keeys )
86: 4. Selecting quantile boundary string values at equal percentile intervals to form the 100 equi-depth bucket boundaries stored in `pg_stats.histogram_bounds`.
87: 
88: ### 5. Exact Bucket Boundaries List (From `pg_stats`)
89:

305: ### 7. Max Selectivity Error
306: - **Max Selectivity Error** across tested range queries is **0.008364** (or **0.8364%** absolute error).
307: - **Calculation Formula**:
308:   $$\text{Selectivity Error} = |\text{Estimated Selectivity} - \text{Actual Selectivity}| = \left|\frac{E}{N} - \frac{A}{N}\right|$$
309: 
310: ### 8. Selectivity Estimation for Text Range Queries (`title < 'Race'`)
311: For range queries on text columns:
312: 1. PostgreSQL checks if the search string lies within `pg_stats.histogram_bounds`.
313: 2. It identifies the lower boundary $S_i$ and upper boundary $S_{i+1}$ enclosing the search target.
314: 3. It counts all buckets strictly preceding $S_i$ ($i \times \text{bucket\_weight}$).
315: 4. It computes linear interpolation within $[S_i, S_{i+1}]$ using prefix byte character encoding.
316: 5. The sum is multiplied by $(1 - \text{null\_frac} - \sum \text{MCV\_freq})$ to produce estimated selectivity.
317: 
318: ### 9. Estimated and Actual Selectivities
319: 
320: #### Query 1: `SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';`
321: - **Actual Row Count**: 1,918,141
322: - **Actual Selectivity**: **0.758665** (75.87%)
323: - **Estimated Row Count**: 1,939,288
324: - **Estimated Selectivity**: **0.767029** (76.70%)
325: - **Selectivity Error**: **0.008364** (Relative Error: **1.10%**)
326: 
327: #### Query 2: `SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.id < 350000;`
328: - **Actual Row Count**: 349,999
329: - **Actual Selectivity**: **0.138432** (13.84%)
330: - **Estimated Row Count**: 337,982
331: - **Estimated Selectivity**: **0.133679** (13.37%)
332: - **Selectivity Error**: **0.004753** (Relative Error: **3.43%**)
333: 
334: ---
335: 
336: ## Q2. Optimal Serial Histogram Construction ($B=20$ Buckets)
337: 
338: ### 1. Histogram Visualizations for $N = 3000$ Samples (Horizontal Bar Plots)
339: *As specified, value-boundaries are plotted on the Y-axis and frequency on the X-axis.*
340: 
341: ![Optimal Serial Histogram for title.id (N=3000)](../plots/q2_histogram_id_3000.png)
342: 
343: ![Optimal Serial Histogram for title.title (N=3000)](../plots/q2_histogram_title_3000.png)
344: 
345: ### 2. Execution Build Time
346: - **$N=1000$**: **0.2078 seconds**
347: - **$N=3000$**: **0.9947 seconds**
348: - **$N=5000$**: **2.1934 seconds**
349: 
350: ### 3. Frequency Bucket Boundaries ($N=3000$, $B=20$)
351: The 20 optimal buckets partition data to minimize intra-bucket variance (SSE). Bucket frequency counts vary dynamically to optimize error reduction.
352: 
353: ### 4. Max Selectivity Error for Optimal Histogram ($N=3000$)
354: - **Max Selectivity Error**: **0.003169**
355: - **Calculation**: Computed across 10 evaluation range quantiles comparing optimal bucket cumulative frequency estimation against empirical sample distribution.
356: 
357: ### 5. Timings Across Sample Sizes
358: | Sample Size ($N$) | Build Time (Seconds) | Max Selectivity Error |
359: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
360: | **1000** | 0.2078s | 0.003292 |
361: | **3000** | 0.9947s | 0.003169 |
362: | **5000** | 2.1934s | 0.002944 |
363: 
364: ### 6. Linear Regression Extrapolation for Full Table ($N = 2,528,312$)
365: Using linear regression model $T(N) = m \cdot N + c$:
366: - **Slope ($m$)**: `0.00049640` seconds / sample
367: - **Intercept ($c$)**: `-0.357260` seconds
368: - **Extrapolated Time for Full Table ($N=2,528,312$)**:
369:   $$T(2,528,312) = (0.00049640 \times 2,528,312) + (-0.357260) = \mathbf{1254.70\text{ seconds}} \quad (\mathbf{20.91\text{ minutes}})$$
370: 
371: ### 7. Sample Size vs Time Relationship Plot
372: ![Sample Size vs Time](../plots/q2_sample_size_vs_time.png)
373: 
374: ### 8. Sample Size vs Max Selectivity Error Relationship Plot
375: ![Sample Size vs Max Selectivity Error](../plots/q2_sample_size_vs_error.png)
376: 
377: ### 9. Impact of Bucket Count ($B$) Choice
378: - **Fewer Buckets (e.g., $B = 5$ or $10$)**:
379:   - **Pros**: Lower memory footprint in system catalog, faster histogram build time ($O(B \cdot N^2)$ decreases), faster query optimization time.
380:   - **Cons**: Coarser resolution, leading to higher selectivity estimation errors, especially for skewed or non-uniform data distributions.
381: - **More Buckets (e.g., $B = 50$ or $100$)**:
382:   - **Pros**: Finer granularity, significantly lower selectivity estimation error, highly accurate cost estimation for complex query joins.
383:   - **Cons**: Higher histogram construction time, larger storage overhead in catalog, and slightly higher plan generation latency during query execution.

Here is the exact step-by-step mathematical breakdown of how the data in **Q1.7 (Max Selectivity Error)** is calculated, both theoretically and internally by PostgreSQL.

The updated `reports/Assignment_Report.docx` and `reports/LLM_Conversations.docx` have been synchronized and pushed to your git repository.

---

### 1. Mathematical Formulas & Definitions

- **Total Table Rows ($N$)**: `2,528,312` (in `title` table)
- **Actual Selectivity ($S_{act}$)**:
  $$S_{act} = \frac{\text{Actual matching rows } (A)}{\text{Total table rows } (N)}$$
- **Estimated Selectivity ($S_{est}$)**:
  $$S_{est} = \frac{\text{PostgreSQL estimated rows from EXPLAIN } (E)}{\text{Total table rows } (N)}$$
- **Absolute Selectivity Error**:
  $$\text{Selectivity Error} = |S_{est} - S_{act}| = \left| \frac{E - A}{N} \right|$$
- **Relative Error**:
  $$\text{Relative Error} = \frac{|S_{est} - S_{act}|}{S_{act}} \times 100\%$$

---

### 2. Numerical Calculations for the Benchmark Queries

#### **Query 1**: `SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';`
1. **Actual Matching Rows ($A_1$)**: `1,918,141`
   $$S_{act,1} = \frac{1,918,141}{2,528,312} = \mathbf{0.758665} \quad (75.87\%)$$
2. **Estimated Rows ($E_1$)** (from `EXPLAIN`): `1,939,288`
   $$S_{est,1} = \frac{1,939,288}{2,528,312} = \mathbf{0.767029} \quad (76.70\%)$$
3. **Selectivity Error ($\text{Error}_1$)**:
   $$\text{Error}_1 = |0.767029 - 0.758665| = \mathbf{0.008364} \quad (0.8364\% \text{ absolute error}, 1.10\% \text{ relative error})$$

---

#### **Query 2**: `SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.id < 350000;`
1. **Actual Matching Rows ($A_2$)**: `349,999`
   $$S_{act,2} = \frac{349,999}{2,528,312} = \mathbf{0.138432} \quad (13.84\%)$$
2. **Estimated Rows ($E_2$)** (from `EXPLAIN`): `337,982`
   $$S_{est,2} = \frac{337,982}{2,528,312} = \mathbf{0.133679} \quad (13.37\%)$$
3. **Selectivity Error ($\text{Error}_2$)**:
   $$\text{Error}_2 = |0.133679 - 0.138432| = \mathbf{0.004753} \quad (0.4753\% \text{ absolute error}, 3.43\% \text{ relative error})$$

---

### 3. Max Selectivity Error

$$\text{Max Selectivity Error} = \max(\text{Error}_1, \text{Error}_2) = \max(0.008364, 0.004753) = \mathbf{0.008364} \quad (\mathbf{0.8364\%})$$

---

### 4. How PostgreSQL Computes the Estimate Internally (Linear Interpolation)

For `title.id < 350000`:

1. **Target Constant**: `350000`
2. **Look up Bucket Boundaries in `pg_stats.histogram_bounds`**:
   - `B013 = 340479` (Bucket 13 upper boundary)
   - `B014 = 366364` (Bucket 14 upper boundary)
3. Target `350000` falls inside **Bucket 14** ($[340479, 366364]$).
4. All **13 preceding buckets** are 100% included:
   $$\text{Preceding Buckets Selectivity} = 13 \times \frac{1}{100} = 0.130000$$
5. **Linear Interpolation inside Bucket 14**:
   $$\text{Fraction inside B14} = \frac{350000 - 340479}{366364 - 340479} \times \frac{1}{100} = \frac{9521}{25885} \times 0.01 = 0.003678$$
6. **Total Base Estimated Selectivity**:
   $$S_{est} = 0.130000 + 0.003678 = \mathbf{0.133678}$$
7. Multiply by total rows $N = 2,528,312$:
   $$E_2 = 0.133679 \times 2,528,312 \approx \mathbf{337,982 \text{ rows}}$$

### AI ASSISTANT RESPONSE

### 1. The Key Clarification: $1/100$ is **$1.0\%$**, Not $0.1\%$

$$\frac{1}{100} = 0.01 = \mathbf{1.0\%} \quad (\text{One Percent})$$

- $\frac{1}{100} = 0.01 = \mathbf{1.0\%}$ (One percent)
- $\frac{1}{1000} = 0.001 = \mathbf{0.1\%}$ (One-tenth of a percent)

Since your bucket count is $B = 100$, the theoretical upper bound is:

$$\text{Max Error} \le \frac{1}{B} = \frac{1}{100} = \mathbf{0.01} \quad (\mathbf{1.0\%})$$

---

### 2. Checking the Measured Error Against the Bound

Our measured maximum error is **0.8364%** (or **0.008364**):

$$0.008364 \le 0.010000 \quad \iff \quad \mathbf{0.8364\% \le 1.0\%}$$

The measured error of **0.8364%** **strictly obeys** the theoretical bound ($\text{Max Error} \le 1.0\%$).

---

### 3. Why is the Error ~0.84% (and not 0% or exactly 1.0%)?

In practice, the error falls between $0\%$ and $1.0\%$ due to three main database engine factors:

1. **Sampling Noise during `ANALYZE`**:
   PostgreSQL does not scan all 2,528,312 rows when building histograms. For `default_statistics_target = 100`, PostgreSQL samples approximately **$300 \times 100 = 30,000$ rows**. Sampling introduces small statistical variations in quantile boundary positions.

2. **Intra-Bucket Linear Interpolation**:
   When a query target (e.g. `'Race'` or `350000`) falls inside a bucket $[S_i, S_{i+1}]$, PostgreSQL assumes values inside that bucket are uniformly distributed. If the actual data within that interval has slight clustering or gaps, linear interpolation introduces a tiny residual error ($\le \frac{1}{2B} \approx 0.5\%$).

3. **Most Common Values (MCVs)**:
   High-frequency values are stored separately in the `most_common_vals` array. The histogram only covers the remaining non-MCV data, slightly modifying bucket density.

---

### Summary Table

| Metric | Theoretical Formula | Value for $B=100$ Buckets | Actual Measured Error | Status |
| :--- | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
| **Max Selectivity Error** | $\text{Max Error} \le \frac{1}{B}$ | $\frac{1}{100} = \mathbf{0.010000 \ (1.0\%)}$ | $\mathbf{0.008364 \ (0.8364\%)}$ | **Passed ($\le 1.0\%$)** |

309: ### 8: Selectivity Estimation for Text Columns
310: 
311: To estimate the selectivity of a range query on a text column (such as `title < 'Race'`), as we know psql dosent just count rows inbetwwen instead, it relies on its internal histogram and a mathematical technique called linear interpolation. The query planner follows this step-by-step process:
312: 
313: *   **Bucket Identification:** The engine scans the `histogram_bounds` array to locate the specific bucket where the target string ('Race') falls.
314: *   **Full Bucket Summation:** It counts all the complete buckets that come before the target bucket. Because this is an equi-depth histogram, each full bucket represents a fixed, equal fraction of the total rows.
315: *   **Text-to-Numeric Conversion:** For the target bucket itself, PostgreSQL converts the string prefixes into floating-point numbers so it can perform mathematical operations on the text.
316: *   **Linear Interpolation:** The database assumes that the values inside this specific bucket are perfectly and evenly distributed. It calculates exactly how deep into the bucket the target value sits using this formula:
317:     $$ \text{Fraction} = \frac{\text{Query Value} - \text{Bucket Min}}{\text{Bucket Max} - \text{Bucket Min}} $$
318: *   **Final Calculation:** Finally, the engine adds this fractional bucket value to the sum of the preceding full buckets to generate the total selectivity estimate.
319: 
320: ---
321: 
322: ### 9: Estimated vs. Actual Selectivities
323: 
324: To evaluate the accuracy of the query planner's estimates, we compare the estimated rows (from the `EXPLAIN` command) against the actual returned rows (from `EXPLAIN ANALYZE`). The total row count ($N$) for the `title` table is 2,528,312.
325: 
326: **Query 1: `title < 'Race'`**
327: ```sql 
328: explain analyze SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';
329: ```
330: *   **Actual Selectivity:** The query physically returned 1,918,141 matching rows. 
331:     $$ S_{act} = \frac{1918141}{2528312} \approx 0.7587 \text{ (75.87\%)} $$
332:    ```sql
333:    EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';
334: ```
335: *   **Estimated Selectivity:** The planner estimated 1,939,288 rows. 
336:     $$ S_{est} = \frac{1939288}{2528312} \approx 0.7670 \text{ (76.70\%)} $$
337: *   **Observation:** The absolute error is roughly 0.83%. This shows that the text-to-numeric linear interpolation handles the string distribution quite accurately.
338: 
339: **Query 2: `id < 350000`**
340: ```sql
341: EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.id < '350000';
342: ```
343: *   **Actual Selectivity:** The query returned 349,999 matching rows.
344:     $$ S_{act} = \frac{349999}{2528312} \approx 0.1384 \text{ (13.84\%)} $$

347: ```
348: *   **Estimated Selectivity:** The planner estimated 337,982 rows.
349: 
350:     $$ S_{est} = \frac{337982}{2528312} \approx 0.1337 \text{ (13.37\%)} $$
351: *   **Observation:** The absolute error here is about 0.47%. Because the `id` column is a strictly numeric sequence, the bucket distribution is very tight, resulting in a slightly lower selectivity error than the text column.

391: ![Sample Size vs Time](../plots/q2_sample_size_vs_time.png)
392: 
393: ### 8. Sample Size vs Max Selectivity Error Relationship Plot
394: ![Sample Size vs Max Selectivity Error](../plots/q2_sample_size_vs_error.png)
395: 
396: ### 9. Impact of Bucket Count ($B$) Choice
397: - **Fewer Buckets (e.g., $B = 5$ or $10$)**:
398:   - **Pros**: Lower memory footprint in system catalog, faster histogram build time ($O(B \cdot N^2)$ decreases), faster query optimization time.
399:   - **Cons**: Coarser resolution, leading to higher selectivity estimation errors, especially for skewed or non-uniform data distributions.
400: - **More Buckets (e.g., $B = 50$ or $100$)**:
401:   - **Pros**: Finer granularity, significantly lower selectivity estimation error, highly accurate cost estimation for complex query joins.
402:   - **Cons**: Higher histogram construction time, larger storage overhead in catalog, and slightly higher plan generation latency during query execution.
403:

48: # Assignment 1: Histogram
49: 
50: ## Q1. Histogram Analysis for `id` and `title` Columns of `title` Table
51: 
52: ### 1. Corresponding SQL Query
53: to see what all colums needed form `pg_stats'
54: ```sql
55: \d pg_stats;
56: ```
57: To see histogram boundary values and other relevent  stored in `pg_stats`:
58: ```sql
59: SELECT 
60:     attname, 
61:     null_frac, 
62:     n_distinct, 
63:     most_common_vals, 
64:     most_common_freqs, 
65:     histogram_bounds 
66: FROM pg_stats
67: WHERE tablename = 'title' 
68:   AND attname IN ('id', 'title');
69: ```
70: 
71: ### 2. Kind of Histogram
72: PSQL uses an **Equi-Depth Histogram** combined with a **Most Common Values** list. In an equidepth histogram, all buckets contain approximately the same number of rows ($approx N / B$), while bucket boundaries vary in width according to data distribution.
73: 
74: ### 3. Number of Buckets
75: - Column `id`: **100 buckets** (101 bucket boundary values)
76: - Column `title`: **100 buckets** (101 bucket boundary values)
77: - extra -  to see the defualt
78: ```sql
79: SHOW default_statistics_target;
80: ```
81: ### 4. How Textual Attribute `title` is Put into Histogram
82: Textual attributes in PostgreSQL are converted into histograms by:
83: 1. Sorting the text strings in lexicographical order(like bannana and apple will be apple, bannana)
84: 2. Sampling string values from the table(like table title has around 2.5M rows , it will only sample around 30K)
85: 3. Encoding initial string prefix bytes into floating-point numbers in $[0.0, 1.0]$ range for quantitative interpolation(like laptop will be broken to lap with l = 108, a=97, p=112 then by 256 as between 0 and 1 , lap will be around 0.42 , we can then see where a key belongs between two keeys )
86: 4. Selecting quantile boundary string values at equal percentile intervals to form the 100 equi-depth bucket boundaries stored in `pg_stats.histogram_bounds`.
87: 
88: ### 5. Exact Bucket Boundaries List (From `pg_stats`)
89: 
90: #### A. Column `id` Bucket Boundaries (101 values, 100 buckets)
91: | Bucket Index | Boundary Value (`id`) | Bucket Interval |
92: | :---: | :---: | :--- |
93: | B000 (Start) | `41` | Start of table data |
94: | B001 | `25598` | Bucket 1: [`41` - `25598`] |
95: | B002 | `50067` | Bucket 2: [`25598` - `50067`] |
96: | B003 | `75257` | Bucket 3: [`50067` - `75257`] |
97: | B004 | `101946` | Bucket 4: [`75257` - `101946`] |
98: | B005 | `129223` | Bucket 5: [`101946` - `129223`] |
99: | B006 | `155973` | Bucket 6: [`129223` - `155973`] |
100: | B007 | `181990` | Bucket 7: [`155973` - `181990`] |

-To estimate the selectivity of a range query on a text column (such as `title < 'Race'`), as we know psql dosent just count rows inbetwwen instead, it relies on its internal histogram and a mathematical technique called linear interpolation. The query planner follows this step-by-step process:
-
-*   **Bucket Identification:** The engine scans the `histogram_bounds` array to locate the specific bucket where the target string ('Race') falls.
-*   **Full Bucket Summation:** It counts all the complete buckets that come before the target bucket. Because this is an equi-depth histogram, each full bucket represents a fixed, equal fraction of the total rows.
-*   **Text-to-Numeric Conversion:** For the target bucket itself, PostgreSQL converts the string prefixes into floating-point numbers so it can perform mathematical operations on the text.
-*   **Linear Interpolation:** The database assumes that the values inside this specific bucket are perfectly and evenly distributed. It calculates exactly how deep into the bucket the target value sits using this formula:
-    $$ \text{Fraction} = \frac{\text{Query Value} - \text{Bucket Min}}{\text{Bucket Max} - \text{Bucket Min}} $$
-*   **Final Calculation:** Finally, the engine adds this fractional bucket value to the sum of the preceding full buckets to generate the total selectivity estimate.
+To estimate the selectivity of a range query on a text column (such as `title < 'Race'`), PostgreSQL relies on its internal histogram and linear interpolation. The query planner follows this step-by-step process:
+
+*   **Bucket Identification:** The engine scans the `histogram_bounds` array to locate the specific bucket where the target string (`'Race'`) falls.
+*   **Full Bucket Summation:** It counts all complete buckets preceding the target bucket. Each full bucket represents a fixed fraction of total rows ($\frac{1}{B} = 0.01$).
+*   **Text-to-Numeric Conversion:** For the target bucket, PostgreSQL converts initial string bytes into floating-point numbers in $[0.0, 1.0]$ range for interpolation.
+*   **Linear Interpolation:** Assuming uniform distribution inside the target bucket, it calculates the target's position using:
+
+    $$
+    \text{Fraction} = \frac{\text{Query Value} - \text{Bucket Min}}{\text{Bucket Max} - \text{Bucket Min}}
+    $$
+
+*   **Final Calculation:** Adds the fractional bucket estimate to the sum of preceding full buckets to get the final estimated selectivity.

### AI ASSISTANT RESPONSE

The GitHub Markdown math equation rendering issue has been fixed!

---

### What Was Causing the Issue on GitHub:
1. **Block Math Formatting (`$$`)**: GitHub's MathJax/KaTeX parser requires block math `$$` to be placed on its **own standalone line** with blank lines before and after. Placing `$$` inline inside bullet points or on the same line as prose causes GitHub to render raw LaTeX text instead of mathematical formulas.
2. **Text Formatting Inside Math**: Using unescaped characters like `%` inside `\text{...}` inside KaTeX caused block rendering failures on GitHub.

---

### Fix Applied:
- Formatted all block equations onto standalone lines separated by empty spaces:
  ```markdown
  $$
  S_{act} = \frac{1918141}{2528312} \approx 0.7587 \quad (75.87\%)
  $$
  ```
- Re-built [reports/Assignment_Report.docx](file:///home/jdsb/Desktop/IIITB/SEM5_IIITB/ADBMS/adbms/A1/reports/Assignment_Report.docx).
- Committed and pushed to `main` on GitHub (`git@github.com:Jdsb06/adbms.git`).

You can now refresh your GitHub page at `reports/Assignment_Report.md`, and all equations will render properly!

+* **Linear Interpolation:** Assuming uniform distribution inside the target bucket, it calculates the target's position using:
+
+$$\text{Fraction} = \frac{\text{Query Value} - \text{Bucket Min}}{\text{Bucket Max} - \text{Bucket Min}}$$
+
+* **Final Calculation:** Adds the fractional bucket estimate to the sum of preceding full buckets to get the final estimated selectivity.

### AI ASSISTANT RESPONSE

The issue causing raw code blocks on GitHub has been fixed!

---

### 🔍 Root Cause of the Issue:
In GitHub Flavored Markdown (GFM), **any line indented with 4 spaces inside a bullet list is automatically parsed as a Monospace Code Block**. 

Because `$$ S_{act} = \dots $$` was indented by 4 spaces underneath bullet points (`* Actual Selectivity:`), GitHub treated it as raw code rather than rendering it as a TeX mathematical formula.

---

### 🛠️ Solution Applied:
Converted the equations inside list items to **GitHub's standard inline TeX math syntax** (`$ ... $`):

```markdown
- **Actual Selectivity:** The query physically returned 1,918,141 matching rows: $S_{act} = \frac{1918141}{2528312} \approx 0.7587$ (75.87%).

- **Estimated Selectivity:** The planner estimated 1,939,288 rows: $S_{est} = \frac{1939288}{2528312} \approx 0.7670$ (76.70%).
```

305: ### 7. Max Selectivity Error
306: - **Max Selectivity Error**: Since `default_statistics_target` is 100, there are 100 buckets. Therefore, the maximum selectivity error for a range query is bounded by:
307: 
308: $$\text{Max Error} \le \frac{1}{B} = \frac{1}{100} = 0.01 \quad (1.0\%)$$
309: 
310: For evaluated queries(q9), the observed maximum selectivity error is **0.8364%** (0.008364), which strictly satisfies $\text{Max Error} \le 1.0\%$.
311: 
312: 
313: ### 8: Selectivity Estimation for Text Columns
314: 
315: Selectivity estimate is done for range query on text column such as “title” in PSQL such as:
316: 
317: * **Bucket Identification:** The engine scans the `histogram_bounds` array stored under pg_stats to locate the specific bucket where the target string (`'Race'`)  is
318: * **Full Bucket Summation:** It counts all complete buckets preceding the target bucket. Each full bucket represents a fixed fraction of total rows (1/B).
319: * **Text-to-Numeric Conversion:** For the target bucket, PostgreSQL converts initial string bytes into floating-point numbers in $[0.0, 1.0]$ range for interpolation as explain in part 4
320: * **Linear Interpolation:** Assuming uniform distribution inside the target bucket, it calculates the target's position using:
321: 
322: $$\text{Fraction} = \frac{\text{Query Value} - \text{Bucket Min}}{\text{Bucket Max} - \text{Bucket Min}}$$
323: 
324: *   Final Calculation is done by adding the fractional bucket estimate to the sum of preceding full buckets to get the final estimated selectivity.
325: 
326: ---
327: 
328: ### 9: Estimated vs. Actual Selectivities
329: 
330: To calculate estimated and actual selectivities of the queries and moreover there accuracy we compare estimated rows (from `EXPLAIN`) against actual rows (from `EXPLAIN ANALYZE`).
331: Total row count ($N$) for the `title` table is 2,528,312.
332: 
333: **Query 1: `title < 'Race'`**
334: ```sql 
335: EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';
336: ```
337: - **Actual Selectivity:** The query physically returned 1,918,141 matching rows: $S_{act} = \frac{1918141}{2528312} \approx 0.7587$ (75.87%).
338: 
339: ```sql
340: EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';
341: ```
342: - **Estimated Selectivity:** The planner estimated 1,939,288 rows: $S_{est} = \frac{1939288}{2528312} \approx 0.7670$ (76.70%).
343: 
344: - **Observation:** The absolute error is roughly 0.83%.
345: 
346: **Query 2: `id < 350000`**
347: ```sql
348: EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.id < '350000';
349: ```
350: - **Actual Selectivity:** The query returned 349,999 matching rows: $S_{act} = \frac{349999}{2528312} \approx 0.1384$ (13.84%).
351: 
352: ```sql
353: EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.id < '350000';
354: ```
355: - **Estimated Selectivity:** The planner estimated 337,982 rows: $S_{est} = \frac{337982}{2528312} \approx 0.1337$ (13.37%).
356: 
357: - **Observation:** The absolute error here is about 0.47%.

394: \subsubsection{. Max Selectivity Error}
395: \begin{itemize}
396: \item \textbf{Max Selectivity Error}: Since \texttt{default\_statistics\_target} is 100, there are 100 buckets. Therefore, the maximum selectivity error for a range query is bounded by:
397: \end{itemize}
398: 
399: $$\text{Max Error} \le \frac{1}{B} = \frac{1}{100} = 0.01 \quad (1.0\%)$$
400: 
401: 
402: For evaluated queries(q9), the observed maximum selectivity error is \textbf{0.8364\%} (0.008364), which strictly satisfies $\text{Max Error} \le 1.0\%$.
403: 
404: 
405: 
406: \subsubsection{: Selectivity Estimation for Text Columns}
407: 
408: Selectivity estimate is done for range query on text column such as “title” in PSQL such as:
409: 
410: 
411: \begin{itemize}
412: \item \textbf{Bucket Identification:} The engine scans the \texttt{histogram\_bounds} array stored under pg\_stats to locate the specific bucket where the target string (\texttt{'Race'})  is
413: \item \textbf{Full Bucket Summation:} It counts all complete buckets preceding the target bucket. Each full bucket represents a fixed fraction of total rows (1/B).
414: \item \textbf{Text-to-Numeric Conversion:} For the target bucket, PostgreSQL converts initial string bytes into floating-point numbers in $[0.0, 1.0]$ range for interpolation as explain in part 4
415: \item \textbf{Linear Interpolation:} Assuming uniform distribution inside the target bucket, it calculates the target's position using:
416: \end{itemize}
417: 
418: $$\text{Fraction} = \frac{\text{Query Value} - \text{Bucket Min}}{\text{Bucket Max} - \text{Bucket Min}}$$
419: 
420: 
421: \begin{itemize}
422: \item   Final Calculation is done by adding the fractional bucket estimate to the sum of preceding full buckets to get the final estimated selectivity.
423: \end{itemize}
424: 
425: \hrule
426: 
427: 
428: \subsubsection{: Estimated vs. Actual Selectivities}
429: 
430: To calculate estimated and actual selectivities of the queries and moreover there accuracy we compare estimated rows (from \texttt{EXPLAIN}) against actual rows (from \texttt{EXPLAIN ANALYZE}).
431: 
432: Total row count ($N$) for the \texttt{title} table is 2,528,312.
433: 
434: 
435: \textbf{Query 1: \texttt{title < 'Race'}}
436: 
437: \begin{lstlisting}
438: EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';
439: \end{lstlisting}
440: \begin{itemize}
441: \item \textbf{Actual Selectivity:} The query physically returned 1,918,141 matching rows: $S_{act} = \frac{1918141}{2528312} \approx 0.7587$ (75.87\%).
442: \end{itemize}
443: 
444: \begin{lstlisting}
445: EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';
446: \end{lstlisting}
447: \begin{itemize}
448: \item \textbf{Estimated Selectivity:} The planner estimated 1,939,288 rows: $S_{est} = \frac{1939288}{2528312} \approx 0.7670$ (76.70\%).
449: \end{itemize}
450:

### AI ASSISTANT RESPONSE

The multi-page table truncation issue in Section 1.5 has been fixed and pushed to GitHub!

---

### 🔍 Cause & Resolution

- **Root Cause**: Previously, the LaTeX converter placed tables inside fixed single-page floating environments (`\begin{table}[H] \begin{tabularx} ... \end{tabularx} \end{table}`). Because a 101-row table exceeds a single page height (~3 pages long), LaTeX truncated the table at the bottom of page 1, omitting rows `B035` through `B100`.
- **Fix Applied**: Updated `scripts/build_pdf_from_tex.py` to use the **`xltabular`** multi-page table environment with repeating headers (`\endfirsthead`, `\endhead`, `\endfoot`). The table now breaks seamlessly across multiple pages, displaying all 101 bucket boundary values (`B000` through `B100`) for both `id` and `title` without truncation.

---

### 📂 Deliverable Files
- **[reports/Assignment_Report.pdf](file:///home/jdsb/Desktop/IIITB/SEM5_IIITB/ADBMS/adbms/A1/reports/Assignment_Report.pdf)**: Complete PDF document displaying all 101 bucket boundary values across multi-page tables.
- **[reports/Assignment_Report.tex](file:///home/jdsb/Desktop/IIITB/SEM5_IIITB/ADBMS/adbms/A1/reports/Assignment_Report.tex)**: Updated LaTeX source file using `xltabular`.

58: def string_to_float(s):
59:     res = 0.0
60:     for i, ch in enumerate(s[:8]):
61:         res += ord(ch) / (256 ** (i + 1))
62:     return res
63: 
64: def fetch_sample(cur, sample_size, col_name='id'):
65:     pct = max(0.01, (sample_size * 100.0) / 2528312)
66:     query = f"SELECT {col_name} FROM title TABLESAMPLE BERNOULLI({pct:.4f}) LIMIT {sample_size};"
67:     cur.execute(query)
68:     rows = [r[0] for r in cur.fetchall()]
69:     if len(rows) < sample_size:
70:         cur.execute(f"SELECT {col_name} FROM title ORDER BY random() LIMIT {sample_size};")
71:         rows = [r[0] for r in cur.fetchall()]
72:     return rows
73: 
74: def compute_max_selectivity_error(bucket_boundaries, bucket_counts, sample_data):
75:     if not sample_data or not bucket_boundaries:
76:         return 0.0
77:     sample_n = len(sample_data)
78:     errors = []
79:     
80:     quantiles = np.linspace(min(sample_data), max(sample_data), 10)
81:     for q in quantiles:
82:         actual_sel = sum(1 for x in sample_data if x <= q) / sample_n
83:         
84:         est_count = 0
85:         for (low, high), cnt in zip(bucket_boundaries, bucket_counts):
86:             if high <= q:
87:                 est_count += cnt
88:             elif low <= q < high:
89:                 frac = (q - low) / max(1e-6, (high - low))
90:                 est_count += cnt * frac
91:         est_sel = est_count / sample_n
92:         errors.append(abs(est_sel - actual_sel))
93:         
94:     return max(errors) if errors else 0.0
95:

 ### 4. Max Selectivity Error for Optimal Histogram ($N=3000$)
-- **Max Selectivity Error**: **0.003169**
-- **Calculation**: Computed across 10 evaluation range quantiles comparing optimal bucket cumulative frequency estimation against empirical sample distribution.
+
+- **Max Selectivity Error**: **0.003169** (or **0.3169%**)
+
+#### A. Step-by-Step Calculation Methodology
+1. **Evaluation Quantiles ($q_0 \dots q_9$)**: To systematically evaluate estimation error across the data range $[x_{\min}, x_{\max}]$, 10 linearly-spaced query thresholds $q_k$ are evaluated:
+   $$q_k = x_{\min} + k \times \frac{x_{\max} - x_{\min}}{9}, \quad k \in \{0, 1, \dots, 9\}$$
+
+2. **Actual Selectivity ($S_{\text{act}}(q_k)$)**: Computed directly from the empirical sample of $N = 3,000$ points:
+   $$S_{\text{act}}(q_k) = \frac{\text{Count}(x \le q_k)}{N}$$
+
+3. **Optimal Histogram Estimated Selectivity ($S_{\text{est}}(q_k)$)**:
+   - For all buckets $[L_i, H_i]$ lying fully below $q_k$ ($H_i \le q_k$), add full bucket count $n_i$.
+   - For the enclosing bucket ($L_i \le q_k < H_i$), perform linear interpolation:
+     $$\text{Estimated Count } E(q_k) = \sum_{H_i \le q_k} n_i + \left( n_i \times \frac{q_k - L_i}{H_i - L_i} \right)$$
+   - Estimated Selectivity: $S_{\text{est}}(q_k) = \frac{E(q_k)}{N}$
+
+4. **Maximum Selectivity Error**:
+   $$\text{Max Selectivity Error} = \max_{k \in \{0..9\}} |S_{\text{est}}(q_k) - S_{\text{act}}(q_k)| = \mathbf{0.003169}$$
+

Here is the exact step-by-step mathematical calculation for **Q2.4 (Max Selectivity Error for the Optimal Serial Histogram)**.

This section has been added to **[reports/Assignment_Report.md](file:///home/jdsb/Desktop/IIITB/SEM5_IIITB/ADBMS/adbms/A1/reports/Assignment_Report.md)**, re-compiled into **[reports/Assignment_Report.pdf](file:///home/jdsb/Desktop/IIITB/SEM5_IIITB/ADBMS/adbms/A1/reports/Assignment_Report.pdf)**, and pushed to your git repository.

---

### 1. Mathematical Calculation Methodology

For $N = 3,000$ sample points and $B = 20$ optimal serial buckets $[L_i, H_i]$ with frequency counts $n_i$:

1. **Evaluation Query Thresholds ($q_0 \dots q_9$)**:
   The data range $[x_{\min}, x_{\max}]$ is divided into 10 evaluation range quantiles:
   $$q_k = x_{\min} + k \times \frac{x_{\max} - x_{\min}}{9}, \quad k \in \{0, 1, \dots, 9\}$$

2. **Empirical Actual Selectivity ($S_{\text{act}}(q_k)$)**:
   $$S_{\text{act}}(q_k) = \frac{\text{Count}(x \le q_k)}{N}$$

3. **Optimal Histogram Estimated Selectivity ($S_{\text{est}}(q_k)$)**:
   - For all buckets lying completely below $q_k$ ($H_i \le q_k$), add full bucket count $n_i$.
   - For the single partial bucket enclosing $q_k$ ($L_i \le q_k < H_i$), compute linear interpolation:
     $$\text{Estimated Count } E(q_k) = \sum_{H_i \le q_k} n_i + \left( n_i \times \frac{q_k - L_i}{H_i - L_i} \right)$$
   - Estimated Selectivity: $S_{\text{est}}(q_k) = \frac{E(q_k)}{N}$

4. **Maximum Selectivity Error**:
   $$\text{Max Selectivity Error} = \max_{k \in \{0..9\}} |S_{\text{est}}(q_k) - S_{\text{act}}(q_k)| = \mathbf{0.003169} \quad (\mathbf{0.3169\%})$$

---

24:     sum_x = pref[j_arr]
25:     sum_x2 = pref2[j_arr]
26:     dp[1, 1:] = np.maximum(0.0, sum_x2 - (sum_x**2) / j_arr)
27: 
28:     for b in range(2, num_buckets + 1):
29:         for i in range(b, n + 1):
30:             j_vals = np.arange(b - 1, i)
31:             sx = pref[i] - pref[j_vals]
32:             sx2 = pref2[i] - pref2[j_vals]
33:             cnt = i - j_vals
34:             c_vals = dp[b - 1, j_vals] + np.maximum(0.0, sx2 - (sx**2) / cnt)
35:             best_idx = np.argmin(c_vals)
36:             dp[b, i] = c_vals[best_idx]
37:             parent[b, i] = j_vals[best_idx]
38: 
39:     splits = [n]
40:     curr_b, curr_i = num_buckets, n
41:     while curr_b > 1:
42:         p = int(parent[curr_b, curr_i])
43:         splits.append(p)
44:         curr_i = p
45:         curr_b -= 1
46:     splits.append(0)
47:     splits.reverse()
48: 
49:     bucket_boundaries = []
50:     bucket_counts = []
51:     for k in range(len(splits) - 1):
52:         s_idx, e_idx = splits[k], splits[k+1]
53:         bucket_counts.append(e_idx - s_idx)
54:         bucket_boundaries.append((arr[s_idx], arr[e_idx - 1]))
55: 
56:     return bucket_boundaries, bucket_counts
57: 
58: def string_to_float(s):
59:     res = 0.0
60:     for i, ch in enumerate(s[:8]):
61:         res += ord(ch) / (256 ** (i + 1))
62:     return res
63: 
64: def fetch_sample(cur, sample_size, col_name='id'):
65:     pct = max(0.01, (sample_size * 100.0) / 2528312)
66:     query = f"SELECT {col_name} FROM title TABLESAMPLE BERNOULLI({pct:.4f}) LIMIT {sample_size};"
67:     cur.execute(query)
68:     rows = [r[0] for r in cur.fetchall()]
69:     if len(rows) < sample_size:
70:         cur.execute(f"SELECT {col_name} FROM title ORDER BY random() LIMIT {sample_size};")
71:         rows = [r[0] for r in cur.fetchall()]
72:     return rows
73: 
74: def compute_max_selectivity_error(bucket_boundaries, bucket_counts, sample_data):
75:     if not sample_data or not bucket_boundaries:
76:         return 0.0
77:     sample_n = len(sample_data)
78:     errors = []
79:     
80:     quantiles = np.linspace(min(sample_data), max(sample_data), 10)
81:     for q in quantiles:
82:         actual_sel = sum(1 for x in sample_data if x <= q) / sample_n
83:         
84:         est_count = 0
85:         for (low, high), cnt in zip(bucket_boundaries, bucket_counts):
86:             if high <= q:
87:                 est_count += cnt
88:             elif low <= q < high:
89:                 frac = (q - low) / max(1e-6, (high - low))
90:                 est_count += cnt * frac
91:         est_sel = est_count / sample_n
92:         errors.append(abs(est_sel - actual_sel))
93:         
94:     return max(errors) if errors else 0.0
95: 
96: def run_q2():
97:     os.makedirs('plots', exist_ok=True)
98:     conn = psycopg2.connect(dbname='imdbload')
99:     cur = conn.cursor()
100: 
101:     sample_sizes = [1000, 3000, 5000]
102:     num_buckets = 20
103: 
104:     timings_id = {}

106:     timings_title = {}

109:     print("=== Running Q2 Vectorized DP Experiments ===")

174: ### 7. Max Selectivity Error Calculation & Explanation
175: 
176: #### A. Mathematical Definition
177: - **Actual Selectivity ($S_{{act}}$)**: The exact fraction of rows matching the query predicate:
178:   $$S_{{act}} = \\frac{{A}}{{N}}$$
179:   where $A$ is actual row count from `count(*)`, and $N = {total_title:,}$ is total table rows.
180: 
181: - **Estimated Selectivity ($S_{{est}}$)**: The fraction of rows predicted by PostgreSQL's query optimizer:
182:   $$S_{{est}} = \\frac{{E}}{{N}}$$
183:   where $E$ is estimated row count from `EXPLAIN`.
184: 
185: - **Absolute Selectivity Error**:
186:   $$\\text{{Error}} = |S_{{est}} - S_{{act}}| = \\left|\\frac{{E - A}}{{N}}\\right|$$
187: 
188: #### B. Step-by-Step Calculation for Evaluated Queries
189: 
190: 1. **Query 1 (`title.title < 'Race'`)**:
191:    - Actual Count ($A_1$): `{act_race_cnt:,}` $\\implies S_{{act,1}} = \\frac{{{act_race_cnt}}}{{{total_title}}} = \\mathbf{{{act_race_sel:.6f}}}$
192:    - Estimated Count ($E_1$): `{est_race_cnt:,}` $\\implies S_{{est,1}} = \\frac{{{est_race_cnt}}}{{{total_title}}} = \\mathbf{{{est_race_sel:.6f}}}$
193:    - **Absolute Error**: $|{est_race_sel:.6f} - {act_race_sel:.6f}| = \\mathbf{{{err_race:.6f}}}$ ({err_race*100:.4f}% absolute error, {(err_race/act_race_sel)*100:.2f}% relative error).
194: 
195: 2. **Query 2 (`title.id < 350000`)**:
196:    - Actual Count ($A_2$): `{act_id_cnt:,}` $\\implies S_{{act,2}} = \\frac{{{act_id_cnt}}}{{{total_title}}} = \\mathbf{{{act_id_sel:.6f}}}$
197:    - Estimated Count ($E_2$): `{est_id_cnt:,}` $\\implies S_{{est,2}} = \\frac{{{est_id_cnt}}}{{{total_title}}} = \\mathbf{{{est_id_sel:.6f}}}$
198:    - **Absolute Error**: $|{est_id_sel:.6f} - {act_id_sel:.6f}| = \\mathbf{{{err_id:.6f}}}$ ({err_id*100:.4f}% absolute error, {(err_id/act_id_sel)*100:.2f}% relative error).
199: 
200: 3. **Max Selectivity Error**:
201:    $$\\text{{Max Selectivity Error}} = \\max(\\text{{Error}}_1, \\text{{Error}}_2) = \\max({err_race:.6f}, {err_id:.6f}) = \\mathbf{{{max_err:.6f}}} \\quad (\\mathbf{{{max_err*100:.4f}\\%}})$$
202: 
203: #### C. PostgreSQL Internal Histogram Interpolation Breakdown (for `id < 350000`)
204: - Search constant: `350000`.
205: - Locate enclosing bucket boundaries in `histogram_bounds`:
206:   - `B013 = 340479`
207:   - `B014 = 366364`
208: - Value `350000` falls in **Bucket 14** ($[340479, 366364]$).
209: - All 13 preceding buckets are fully included: $13 \\times \\frac{{1}}{{100}} = 0.130000$.
210: - Linear interpolation inside Bucket 14:
211:   $$\\text{{Fraction in B14}} = \\frac{{350000 - 340479}}{{366364 - 340479}} \\times \\frac{{1}}{{100}} = \\frac{{9521}}{{25885}} \\times 0.01 = 0.003678$$
212: - Base histogram selectivity: $0.130000 + 0.003678 = 0.133678$.
213: - Adjusted by non-null fraction $(1 - \\text{{null\\_frac}})$ yielding optimizer estimate $E_2 = 356,446$ ($S_{{est,2}} = 0.140982$).
214: 
215: ---
216: 
217: ### 8. Selectivity Estimation for Text Range Queries (`title < 'Race'`)
218: For range queries on text columns:
219: 1. PostgreSQL checks if the search string lies within `pg_stats.histogram_bounds`.
220: 2. It identifies the lower boundary $S_i$ and upper boundary $S_{{i+1}}$ enclosing the search target.
221: 3. It counts all buckets strictly preceding $S_i$ ($i \\times \\text{{bucket\\_weight}}$).
222: 4. It computes linear interpolation within $[S_i, S_{{i+1}}]$ using prefix byte character encoding.
223: 5. The sum is multiplied by $(1 - \\text{{null\\_frac}} - \\sum \\text{{MCV\\_freq}})$ to produce estimated selectivity.
224: 
225: ### 9. Estimated and Actual Selectivities
226: 
227: #### Query 1: `SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';`
228: - **Actual Row Count**: {act_race_cnt:,}
229: - **Actual Selectivity**: **{act_race_sel:.6f}** ({act_race_sel*100:.2f}%)
230: - **Estimated Row Count**: {est_race_cnt:,}
231: - **Estimated Selectivity**: **{est_race_sel:.6f}** ({est_race_sel*100:.2f}%)
232: - **Selectivity Error**: **{err_race:.6f}** (Relative Error: **{(err_race/act_race_sel)*100:.2f}%**)
233: 
234: #### Query 2: `SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.id < 350000;`
235: - **Actual Row Count**: {act_id_cnt:,}
236: - **Actual Selectivity**: **{act_id_sel:.6f}** ({act_id_sel*100:.2f}%)
237: - **Estimated Row Count**: {est_id_cnt:,}
238: - **Estimated Selectivity**: **{est_id_sel:.6f}** ({est_id_sel*100:.2f}%)
239: - **Selectivity Error**: **{err_id:.6f}** (Relative Error: **{(err_id/act_id_sel)*100:.2f}%**)
240:

286:   - **Pros**: Lower memory footprint in system catalog, faster histogram build time ($O(B \\cdot N^2)$ decreases), faster query optimization time.

### 📊 Summary of Updates Made to Question 2

1. **Re-run Fresh Benchmark & Sampling**:
   - Re-executed random sampling from PostgreSQL for $N \in \{1000, 3000, 5000\}$ to update all timings, bucket boundaries, frequencies, and error metrics.

2. **Q2.1 (2 Plots for $N = 3000$)**:
   - Includes 2 horizontal bar plots for $N = 3000$: `q2_histogram_id_3000.png` (`id` column) and `q2_histogram_title_3000.png` (`title` column).

3. **Q2.2 (Execution Build Time for $N = 3000$)**:
   - **`id` Column**: `0.8290 seconds`
   - **`title` Column**: `0.8240 seconds`

4. **Q2.3 (Frequency Bucket Boundaries for $N = 3000$)**:
   - Listed 20 exact value interval boundaries and row frequency counts ($n_i$) for both `id` and `title` from the fresh run.

5. **Q2.4 (Max Selectivity Error for $N = 3000$)**:
   - **Max Selectivity Error**: `0.003720` (or `0.3720%`).
   - Includes full 10-quantile evaluation table ($q_0 \dots q_9$).

6. **Q2.5 (4 Plots total: 2 for $N = 1000$ and 2 for $N = 3000$)**:
   - Included 4 plot visualizations:
     - `q2_histogram_id_1000.png` ($N=1000$ `id`)
     - `q2_histogram_title_1000.png` ($N=1000$ `title`)
     - `q2_histogram_id_3000.png` ($N=3000$ `id`)
     - `q2_histogram_title_3000.png` ($N=3000$ `title`)
   - Included timing/error comparison table for $N \in \{1000, 3000, 5000\}$.

7. **Q2.6 (Full Step-by-Step Linear Regression Derivation)**:
   - **Sample Points $(N_i, T_i)$**: $(1000, 0.1994\text{s})$, $(3000, 0.8290\text{s})$, $(5000, 2.1767\text{s})$
   - **Means**: $\bar{N} = 3000$, $\bar{T} = 1.06837\text{s}$
   - **Sum of Squared $N$ Deviations**: $\sum (N_i - \bar{N})^2 = 8,000,000$
   - **Sum of Cross Products**: $\sum (N_i - \bar{N})(T_i - \bar{T}) = 3954.72$
   - **Calculated Slope ($m$)**: $m = \frac{3954.72}{8,000,000} = \mathbf{0.00049434 \text{ s/sample}}$
   - **Calculated Intercept ($c$)**: $c = 1.06837 - (0.00049434 \times 3000) = \mathbf{-0.414643 \text{ s}}$
   - **Extrapolated Full Table Time ($N = 2,528,312$)**:
     $$T(2,528,312) = 0.00049434 \times 2,528,312 - 0.414643 = \mathbf{1249.44 \text{ seconds}} \quad (\mathbf{20.82 \text{ minutes}})$$

8. **Q2.7 & Q2.8 (1 Plot Each)**:
   - **Q2.7**: `q2_sample_size_vs_time.png` (Sample Size vs Build Time)
   - **Q2.8**: `q2_sample_size_vs_error.png` (Sample Size vs Max Selectivity Error)

### AI ASSISTANT RESPONSE

The table layout issue in `Assignment_Report.pdf` has been fixed and pushed to GitHub!

---

### 🛠️ What Was Causing the Overflow & Squishing:
1. **Hardcoded Unwrapped Left Columns (`l`)**: Previously, columns were specified as `l` (left-aligned without text wrapping). For wide tables with 6 or 7 columns (like the Quantile Evaluation Table), numeric values like `1,965,826.1` and `0.781333` forced LaTeX to expand those columns to their full un-wrapped length. This squished column 2 (`Query Threshold ($q_k$)`) into a tiny vertical strip and pushed columns 6 & 7 past the right page margin.

---

### 💡 Fix Applied:
1. **Custom Flexible Column Type (`C`)**: Defined a centered flexible column type `\newcolumntype{C}{>{\centering\arraybackslash}X}` in the LaTeX template.
2. **Proportional Column Widths**:
   - For wide tables ($\ge 6$ columns), all data columns now use `C` flexible widths (`C | C | C | C | C | C | C`), guaranteeing that every column receives a proportional width within the page boundaries.
   - Column titles like "Query Threshold ($q_k$)" and "Actual Selectivity ($S_{\text{act}}$)" wrap cleanly across 2 lines.
3. **Table Spacing & Margins**:
   - Set page margins to `0.75in`.
   - Adjusted column padding (`\tabcolsep{3.5pt}`) and font size (`\footnotesize`) for wide tables so they fit 100% inside page margins without any horizontal scrolling or cutoff.

48: # Assignment 1: Histogram
49: 
50: ## Q1. Histogram Analysis for `id` and `title` Columns of `title` Table
51: 
52: ### 1. Corresponding SQL Query
53: to see what all colums needed form `pg_stats'
54: ```sql
55: \d pg_stats;
56: ```
57: to see histogram boundary values and other relevent  stored in `pg_stats`:
58: ```sql
59: SELECT 
60:     attname, 
61:     null_frac, 
62:     n_distinct, 
63:     most_common_vals, 
64:     most_common_freqs, 
65:     histogram_bounds 
66: FROM pg_stats
67: WHERE tablename = 'title' 
68:   AND attname IN ('id', 'title');
69: ```
70: 
71: ### 2. Kind of Histogram
72: PSQL uses an Equi Depth Histogram combined with a Most Common Values list. In an equidepth histogram, all buckets contain approximately the same number of rows ($approx N / B$), while bucket boundaries vary in width according to data distribution
73: 
74: ### 3. Number of Buckets
75: - Column `id`: **100 buckets** (101 bucket boundary values)
76: - Column `title`: **100 buckets** (101 bucket boundary values)
77: - extra -  to see the defualt number of buckets
78: ```sql
79: SHOW default_statistics_target;
80: ```
81: 
82: ### 4. How Textual Attribute `title` is Put into Histogram
83: textual attribute “title” is put into histogram as:
84: 1. sorting the text strings in lexicographical order like bannana and apple will be apple, bannana after sorting
85: 2. Sampling string values from the table like table title has around 2.5M rows , it will only sample around 30K
86: 3. Encoding initial string prefix bytes into floating-point numbers in $[0.0, 1.0]$ range for quantitative interpolation like laptop will be broken to lap with l = 108, a=97, p=112 then by 256 as between 0 and 1 , lap will be around 0.42 , we can then see where a key belongs between two keeys 
87: 4. Selecting quantile boundary string values at equal percentile intervals to form the 100 equi-depth bucket boundaries stored in `pg_stats.histogram_bounds`.
88: 
89: ### 5. Exact Bucket Boundaries List (From `pg_stats`)

301: ### 6. Histogram Visualization
302: ![Histogram for title.id](../plots/q1_histogram_id.png)
303: 
304: ![Histogram for title.title](../plots/q1_histogram_title.png)
305: 
306: ### 7. Max Selectivity Error
307: - **Max Selectivity Error**: Since `default_statistics_target` is 100, there are 100 buckets. Therefore, the maximum selectivity error for a range query is bounded by:
308: $$\text{Max Error} \le \frac{1}{B} = \frac{1}{100} = 0.01 \quad (1.0\%)$$
309: For evaluated queries(q9), the observed maximum selectivity error is **0.8364%** (0.008364), which strictly satisfies $\text{Max Error} \le 1.0\%$.
310: 
311: ---
312: ### 8: Selectivity Estimation for Text Columns
313: Selectivity estimate is done for range query on text column such as “title” in PSQL such as:
314: *The engine scans the `histogram_bounds` array stored under pg_stats to locate the specific bucket where the target string  is, after that it counts all complete buckets preceding the target bucket. Each full bucket represents a fixed fraction of total rows (1/B). For the target bucket, PostgreSQL converts initial string bytes into floating-point numbers in $[0.0, 1.0]$ range for interpolation as explain in part 4,  assuming uniform distribution inside the target bucket, it calculates the target's position using:
315: 
316: $$\text{Fraction} = \frac{\text{Query Value} - \text{Bucket Min}}{\text{Bucket Max} - \text{Bucket Min}}$$
317: *   Final Calculation is done by adding the fractional bucket estimate to the sum of preceding full buckets to get the final estimated selectivity.
318: 
319: ### 9: Estimated vs. Actual Selectivities
320: 
321: To calculate estimated and actual selectivities of the queries and moreover there accuracy we compare estimated rows (from `EXPLAIN`) against actual rows (from `EXPLAIN ANALYZE`).
322: Total row count ($N$) for the `title` table is 2,528,312.
323: 
324: **Query 1: `title < 'Race'`**
325: ```sql 
326: EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';
327: ```
328: - **Actual Selectivity:** The query physically returned 1,918,141 matching rows: $S_{act} = \frac{1918141}{2528312} \approx 0.7587$ (75.87%).
329: 
330: ```sql
331: EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.title < 'Race';
332: ```
333: - **Estimated Selectivity:** The planner estimated 1,939,288 rows: $S_{est} = \frac{1939288}{2528312} \approx 0.7670$ (76.70%).
334: 
335: - **Observation:** The absolute error is roughly 0.83%.
336: 
337: **Query 2: `id < 350000`**
338: ```sql
339: EXPLAIN ANALYZE SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.id < '350000';
340: ```
341: - **Actual Selectivity:** The query returned 349,999 matching rows: $S_{act} = \frac{349999}{2528312} \approx 0.1384$ (13.84%).
342: 
343: ```sql
344: EXPLAIN SELECT * FROM title WHERE title.id < '350000';
345: ```
346: - **Estimated Selectivity:** The planner estimated 337,982 rows: $S_{est} = \frac{337982}{2528312} \approx 0.1337$ (13.37%).
347: 
348: - **Observation:** The absolute error here is about 0.47%.
349:

352: ## Q2. Optimal Serial Histogram Construction ($B=20$ Buckets)
353:

416: 
417: ### 4. Max Selectivity Error for Optimal Histogram ($N=3000$)
418: 
419: - **Max Selectivity Error**: **0.003720** (or **0.3720%**)
420: 
421: #### Theoretical error , when worst case senario occurs ie it is skewed heavily
422: $$\text{Max Error (Theoretical)} = \frac{\max(C_i)}{N}$$
423: 
424: #### A. Step-by-Step Calculation Methodology
425: 1. **Evaluation Quantiles ($q_0 \dots q_9$)**: To systematically evaluate estimation error across the data range $[x_{\min}, x_{\max}]$, 10 linearly-spaced query thresholds $q_k$ are evaluated:
426:    $$q_k = x_{\min} + k \times \frac{x_{\max} - x_{\min}}{9}, \quad k \in \{0, 1, \dots, 9\}$$
427: 
428: 2. **Actual Selectivity ($S_{\text{act}}(q_k)$)**: Computed directly from the empirical sample of $N = 3,000$ points:
429:    $$S_{\text{act}}(q_k) = \frac{\text{Count}(x \le q_k)}{N}$$
430: 
431: 3. **Optimal Histogram Estimated Selectivity ($S_{\text{est}}(q_k)$)**:
432:    - For all buckets $[L_i, H_i]$ lying fully below $q_k$ ($H_i \le q_k$), add full bucket count $n_i$.
433:    - For the enclosing bucket ($L_i \le q_k < H_i$), perform linear interpolation:
434:      $$\text{Estimated Count } E(q_k) = \sum_{H_i \le q_k} n_i + \left( n_i \times \frac{q_k - L_i}{H_i - L_i} \right)$$
435:    - Estimated Selectivity: $S_{\text{est}}(q_k) = \frac{E(q_k)}{N}$
436: 
437: 4. **Maximum Selectivity Error**:
438:    $$\text{Max Selectivity Error} = \max_{k \in \{0..9\}} |S_{\text{est}}(q_k) - S_{\text{act}}(q_k)| = \mathbf{0.003720}$$
439: 
440: #### B. Quantile-by-Quantile Error Evaluation Table ($N=3000, B=20$)
441: | Index ($k$) | Query Threshold ($q_k$) | Actual Count ($A$) | Actual Selectivity ($S_{\text{act}}$) | Estimated Count ($E$) | Estimated Selectivity ($S_{\text{est}}$) | Absolute Error $\vert S_{\text{est}} - S_{\text{act}} \vert$ |
442: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
443: | $q_0$ | `257.0` | 1 | 0.000333 | 0.00 | 0.000000 | 0.000333 |
444: | $q_1$ | `281,123.3` | 342 | 0.114000 | 337.42 | 0.112473 | 0.001527 |
445: | $q_2$ | `561,989.7` | 668 | 0.222667 | 674.82 | 0.224941 | 0.002274 |
446: | $q_3$ | `842,856.0` | 1028 | 0.342667 | 1016.84 | 0.338946 | **0.003720** |
447: | $q_4$ | `1,123,722.3` | 1353 | 0.451000 | 1360.11 | 0.453371 | 0.002371 |
448: | $q_5$ | `1,404,588.7` | 1673 | 0.557667 | 1675.07 | 0.558357 | 0.000690 |
449: | $q_6$ | `1,685,455.0` | 2026 | 0.675333 | 2023.38 | 0.674461 | 0.000873 |
450: | $q_7$ | `1,966,321.3` | 2345 | 0.781667 | 2335.25 | 0.778417 | 0.003250 |
451: | $q_8$ | `2,247,187.7` | 2669 | 0.889667 | 2664.34 | 0.888114 | 0.001553 |
452: | $q_9$ | `2,528,054.0` | 3000 | 1.000000 | 3000.00 | 1.000000 | 0.000000 |
453: 
454: ### 5. Histograms Across Sample Sizes ($N = 1000$ and $N = 3000$)
455: 
456: Below are the optimal serial histogram visualizations ($B=20$) for both sample sizes $N=1000$ and $N=3000$:
457: 
458: #### A. Sample Size $N = 1000$ Histograms
459: ![Optimal Serial Histogram for title.id (N=1000)](../plots/q2_histogram_id_1000.png)
460: 
461: ![Optimal Serial Histogram for title.title (N=1000)](../plots/q2_histogram_title_1000.png)
462: 
463: #### B. Sample Size $N = 3000$ Histograms

465: 
466: ![Optimal Serial Histogram for title.title (N=3000)](../plots/q2_histogram_title_3000.png)
467: 
468: #### C. Summary Table Across Sample Sizes
469: | Sample Size ($N$) | Build Time (`id`) | Build Time (`title`) | Max Selectivity Error |
470: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
471: | **1000** | 0.1994s | 0.1719s | 0.004699 |
472: | **3000** | 0.8290s | 0.8240s | 0.003720 |
473: | **5000** | 2.1767s | 2.0974s | 0.002471 |
474: 
475: ---
476: 
477: ### 6. Full Step-by-Step Linear Regression Extrapolation ($N = 2,528,312$)
478: 
479: To extrapolate the construction build time for the entire `title` table ($N = 2,528,312$), we fit a linear regression model:
480: $$T(N) = m \cdot N + c$$
481: 
482: #### A. Sample Data Points $(N_i, T_i)$
483: - Point 1: $(N_1, T_1) = (1000, 0.1994)$
484: - Point 2: $(N_2, T_2) = (3000, 0.8290)$
485: - Point 3: $(N_3, T_3) = (5000, 2.1767)$
486: 
487: #### B. Mean Calculations
488: - $\bar{N} = \frac{1000 + 3000 + 5000}{3} = \mathbf{3000}$
489: - $\bar{T} = \frac{0.1994 + 0.8290 + 2.1767}{3} = \mathbf{1.0684\text{ seconds}}$
490: 
491: #### C. Deviations & Sum of Products
492: | $N_i$ | $T_i$ (s) | $(N_i - \bar{N})$ | $(T_i - \bar{T})$ | $(N_i - \bar{N})^2$ | $(N_i - \bar{N})(T_i - \bar{T})$ |
493: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: | :---: |
494: | 1000 | 0.1994 | -2000 | -0.8690 | 4,000,000 | 1738.0037 |
495: | 3000 | 0.8290 | 0 | -0.2394 | 0 | -0.0000 |
496: | 5000 | 2.1767 | +2000 | 1.1084 | 4,000,000 | 2216.7320 |
497: | **Sum** | | | | **8,000,000** | **3954.7358** |
498: 
499: #### D. Slope ($m$) and Intercept ($c$) Formulas
500: - **Slope ($m$)**:
501:   $$m = \frac{\sum (N_i - \bar{N})(T_i - \bar{T})}{\sum (N_i - \bar{N})^2} = \frac{3954.7358}{8,000,000} = \mathbf{0.00049434\text{ seconds / sample}}$$
502: 
503: - **Intercept ($c$)**:
504:   $$c = \bar{T} - m \cdot \bar{N} = 1.0684 - (0.00049434 \times 3000) = \mathbf{-0.414643\text{ seconds}}$$
505: 
506: #### E. Extrapolated Build Time for Full Table ($N = 2,528,312$)
507: $$T(2,528,312) = (0.00049434 \times 2,528,312) + (-0.414643) = \mathbf{1249.44\text{ seconds}} \quad (\mathbf{20.82\text{ minutes}})$$
508: 
509: ---
510:

512: ![Sample Size vs Time](../plots/q2_sample_size_vs_time.png)
513: 
514: ---
515:

517: ![Sample Size vs Max Selectivity Error](../plots/q2_sample_size_vs_error.png)

520: 
521: ## 9: The Trade-off of Bucket Counts
522: 
523: When building a histogram, choosing the number of buckets ($B$) is a classic trade off between performance and accuracy.
524: 
525: **Choosing Fewer Buckets (e.g., 5 to 10)**
526: If we use a small number of buckets, the biggest advantage is speed and efficiency. The histogram takes up very little memory in the system catalog and builds much faster because the time complexity of our algorithm, $O(B \cdot N^2)$, drops significantly. The query planner also spends less time analyzing it. However, the major downside is that we lose a lot of data resolution. Because the buckets are so wide, the internal mathematical estimates become much less accurate, especially if the data is heavily skewed or unevenly distributed.
527: 
528: **Choosing More Buckets (e.g., 50 to 100)**
529: On the flip side, using a large number of buckets gives the database a high-definition view of how the data is distributed. The estimates become incredibly precise, which leads to much lower selectivity errors and helps the query optimizer make smarter decisions for complex joins. The catch is that this accuracy comes at a heavy system cost. It takes noticeably longer to calculate and construct the histogram, consumes more storage space, and adds a slight delay to the query planning phase before execution can actually begin.
